## AI Research

### [San Francisco State University in Twilight](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Y3G26fHXYM3SKYEHh/san-francisco-state-university-in-twilight)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <blockquote>
<p>You will agree that for a woman who has fought three duels, all of them kills, indulging in such fears is ludicrous. But what's around the corner now is a duel so big that I don't think I have the guts for it; in Faust's words: <em>Verweile doch, du bist so schoen!</em> Keep it as it is. Don't change. [...] Take my life but don't take away the meaning of my life.</p>
<p>—<a href="https://www.lexal.net/scifi/scifiction/classics/classics_archive/russ/russ1.html">"When It Changed"</a> by Joanna Russ</p>
</blockquote>
<p>I've been crashing Prof. Lai's "Advanced Topics" class this semester, on Fourier analysis plus enough functional analysis to get by (since the University can't afford to offer functional analysis as a regular class anymore). It's not obviously a good use of my time, but now that I've started, I have to see it through. I could try to tell a story about <em>impact</em>: I am leveling up my math chops, which will be useful for understanding natural abstractions or singular learning theory or mechanistic explanation, which will be useful for—but maybe it's less embarrassing to not try to sell a story. Call it luxury consumption during garbage time. I've lived a good life, in some ways if not others. I could choose to drown in oceans of regret over all the songs I never finished and stories I never wrote and math I never learned when I was young, in the before-time when there was still enough clock in the timeline for these things to matter—or I could choose to do something with the time I have now, which doesn't.</p>
<p>On Wednesday 16 September, the class had its first examination. (When I had dropped by office hours on Monday, Prof. Lai had said I could take it, but that he might not grade it.) I hadn't prepped enough and did poorly, blanking on two parts. I didn't hand in the paper, but GPT-6 Astra later gave it 58%.</p>
<p>After enjoying lunch and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/R7jsxihAzvd6Gf24R/dance-dance">dancing</a> at the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonestown_Galleria">mall</a>, I walked back to campus for the math department's occasional <a href="https://sites.google.com/view/sfsuagc/fall-2026">seminar</a>. On a whim, I had recently ordered a custom branded department tee (in Hebrew, with the thought of provoking a reaction from the other analysis professor, who is a big free-Palestine guy), which I was sure to wear for the occasion. (Ordering custom T-shirts online was already a thing in the before-time, but my modest <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GIMP">GIMP</a> skills would not have yielded as attractive a result in as little effort.) Aida Abiad of the Eindhoven University of Technology was speaking on coding theory applications of spectral graph theory, a topic of nonzero interest to me. (In October 2009, I had ordered a copy of <em>An Introduction to the Theory of Graph Spectra</em>, which sat mostly untouched among my textbook collection for the intervening seventeen years.)</p>
<p>The talk was pretty accessible, as these things go. The independence number  of a graph is the maximum size of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_set_(graph_theory)">a set of non-adjacent vertices</a>. The exact independence number of a graph is <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NP-hardness">hard to compute</a>, but back in the 1970s, people had come up with some upper bounds in terms of the <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/blog/2012/Dec/eigencritters/">eigenvalues</a> of the graph's <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjacency_matrix">adjacency matrix</a>. Abiad explained her work on extending such techniques to bound the <em>k</em>-independence number  (the maximum size of a set of vertices with graph distance greater than <em>k</em> from each other). Once you have a bound in terms of powers of the eigenvalues, it's natural to ask, what about a polynomial?—and then, which polynomial is the best? Then there are applications: if you're constructing a code and a metric on your set of possible codewords is a graph distance, then the <em>k</em>-independence number of the graph tells you about the largest code you can construct whose codewords are greater than <em>k</em> distance apart.</p>
<p>For the most part, it felt like that talk in that room could have taken place any time in the past seventeen or twenty-seven years. (Much further back than that, and it's harder to believe that SFSU would have had a digital projector rather than still being on <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_(projection)">transparencies</a>.) Abiad discussed bound improvements published in 2016, 2019, and 2022, but as far as I could tell as a non-specialist, there was no reason the same results couldn't have been reached in 2006 or 2009 or 2012 except that no one had gotten around to it yet. Besides the paper dates, the relative timelessness of the moment was only betrayed by a few odd remarks Abiad made, like, "Now probably the artificial intelligence gives you the graph invariants if you ask for it."</p>
<p>Afterwards, I took the 28 bus back to Daly City BART station. At the station, the bus stopped before pulling all the way up to the curb. Apparently a car was in the way: the bus driver honked a few times, then more frequently, then in one long, continuous honk. Why wasn't the car moving? "It's a Waymo!" one of the passengers said. "Fucking Waymo," said someone else. "Bring back human drivers!" said a man. The bus driver gave up and opened the doors, still short of the curb. Walking to the station among the throng, I saw that the offending Waymo had moved out of the way; it was unclear what had aroused it to motion.</p>
<p>On the train home, inspiration struck. Recently, one Levent Alpöge of Anthropic (and before that, I think someplace called "Harvert") had been in everyone's feed for Tweeting out LLM-assisted solutions to famous open problems. I didn't have access to the frontier magic that Alpöge had inside Anthropic's walled castle, but I wondered if there was more low-hanging fruit accessible to the public models that hadn't been plucked yet just because there were few enough math people monitoring the AI situation that no one had spent the tokens yet. (My impression from hanging out at SFSU had been that everyone knew about LLMs as a way for students to cheat on homework—that's why Prof. Lai couldn't give take-home exams anymore—or for researchers to look up graph invariants, but that few were extrapolating to an AI-dominated future.)</p>
<p>When I got home, I tasked Claude Opus 5 in Claude Code with going through the papers of Prof. Emily Clader (who had let me <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/docs/algebraic_topology-01.pdf">crash her "Topics" class on algebraic topology this time last year</a>) to look for "open problems that might be interesting to her that might be scoopable with an LLM (so, like, stuff where you can compute a checkable counterexample, which would have been a master's student project)". I said that scooping the result would "probably demand expensive Fable tokens, although Opus is probably fine" for the literature search.</p>
<p>What transpired next happened quickly enough that I can only reconstruct it from the Claude Code session log—with the help of <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/docs/session_2026-09-16_proof-annotated.html">annotations from</a> Claude Opus 5.5 (released 22 September), since the raw JSONL is so unpleasant to read.</p>
<p>Opus 5 downloaded the LaTeX sources of Clader's papers from the ArXiv and grepped for phrases like "open problem", "conjecture" and "future work." "Found a promising one already", it reported after 84 seconds: Clader, Damiolini, Li, and Ramadas 2023 (revised 2025) <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.05463v3">"Wonderful Compactifications and Rational Curves with Cyclic Action"</a> "conjectures that the Betti numbers of  are log-concave."</p>
<p>It continued reading abstracts and grepping for open-problem prompts in a few other papers for another 48 seconds, then decided, "I'll focus on the log-concavity conjecture since it's explicit, computable, and only lightly checked in the original table." Conjecture 4.15 in the paper had been preceded by a table of the aforementioned Betti numbers for eleven values of 2 ≤ <em>r</em> ≤ 4 and 3 ≤ <em>n</em> ≤ 7. "First I'll reproduce their table before searching for any later resolutions." (These words appear in a <code>"type":"thinking"</code> entry in the log, presumably written by a summarizer model based on Opus 5's original chain of thought, which isn't exposed to users.)</p>
<p>Four seconds later, it had written a Python script to reproduce the table, which succeeded. It took another eight seconds to write another Python script to check log-concavity up to <em>r</em> ≤ 15 and <em>n</em> ≤ 40, and "real-rootedness, which implies it" up to <em>r</em> ≤ 15 and <em>n</em> ≤ 25. That program took half a minute to run. "No counterexamples turned up", said the thinking summary, "every case is log-concave and real-rooted, so real-rootedness likely implies log-concavity here. I'll check if this result already exists before attempting a proof."</p>
<p>In parallel with web search and Semantic Scholar citations failing to turn up an existing proof, it did a web search for <code>Athanasiadis binomial Eulerian polynomials colored permutations real-rooted</code>, which turned up <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.00434">a paper on that topic by the named Athanasiadis</a> and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.01278">another by Haglund and Zhang</a> building off of Athanasiadis. "I see this differs from" the <em>r</em> = 2 case in Clader <em>et al.</em>, said the thinking summary on reading the former, "but the same technique applies."</p>
<p>It took another seven seconds to write a Python script to search for a recurrence: given the actual Betti numbers and a guess about what form of recurrence might generate them, finding the coëfficients of the recurrence (if the guess was right) becomes a standard linear algebra computation.</p>
<p>The script crashed. Opus 5 fixed the bug, but the script didn't turn up anything. "Rather than widen the search blindly," reported Opus 5, it would read the Haglund and Zhang paper. "Their methods may apply directly." Its first attempt to locate the theorem statements in the LaTeX of the Haglund and Zhang paper failed, both because it was grepping for "theorem" (and "lemma", "proposition", "conjecture", <em>&amp;c.</em>) whereas the paper used an abbreviation "thm" defined in the preamble, and because the file contained an invalid UTF-8 byte sequence, causing it to be interpreted as a binary file and ignored by Claude Code's version of <code>grep</code>. After converting the file encoding and adjusting the query, Opus 5 was able to read from the paper and write another Python script to check whether the Betti numbers matched anything from the Haglund and Zhang paper: no dice.</p>
<p>Opus 5 thought for 32 seconds. It spent 7 seconds writing another Python script searching for a recurrence, this time using specific numbers for <em>r</em> rather than treating it symbolically. The script took 13 seconds to run and succeeded. "Next I'll derive this recurrence symbolically from the generating function to confirm it rigorously rather than relying on the fit," said the thinking summary. It wrote two more scripts and did some additional web research on whether the conjecture had been resolved elsewhere, then spent 20 seconds writing up a Markdown file of the result and another few seconds on an edit.</p>
<p>"I think we scooped something," it reported, nine minutes and twenty seconds after I had told it to start working. ("We"!)</p>
<p>Back in the human world, I was flabbergasted. "I'll try to read that, but ... that took less than ten minutes of sidereal time!" I wrote. I hadn't even needed to spring for Fable credits.</p>
<p>Basking in the afterglow, I chatted with the model a little more. I asked how long it would have taken me or a typical SFSU master's student to do this. (It said SFSU was unusually strong in this area: a student of Prof. Beck or Prof. Ardila might well have seen real-rootedness.) I asked if it could keep going. (It added a Part II to its writeup, generalizing the result to the setting of a different Clader <em>et al.</em> paper on multimatroids.)</p>
<p>But I knew my work was just beginning. If I wanted to tell Prof. Clader about the solved conjecture without making a fool of myself, I'd need to verify it personally. (There are too many people in the world who blindly trust LLM slop when they shouldn't, so <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zjgJ9gcMKATFdN48K/so-you-don-t-trust-me">you have to pay a costly signal to prove you're not one of them</a>, even in a case where the output actually is trustworthy with the current models, if not last year's models.)</p>
<p>With some intensive tutoring from other Claude instances, I composed <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/docs/betti_numbers_form_a_log-concave_sequence.pdf">my own writeup</a> of <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/docs/log_concavity_Lrn.pdf">the original Opus 5 proof</a>. Other than the fact that Betti numbers measure how many "<em>d</em>-dimensional holes" a space has (where the hole in a ring is a 1-dimensional hole, but the empty middle of a hollow sphere is a 2-dimensional hole), I didn't know anything about the algebraic geometry that the paper was about, but Conjecture 4.15 turned out to be pretty detachable from the algebraic-geometrical context.  is a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_abelian_group">free abelian group</a>—basically a product of some number of copies of the integers. The Betti numbers of the conjecture are the number of copies of the integers in  as <em>k</em> varies for a given <em>r</em> and <em>n</em>.</p>
<p>The authors had shown in Theorem 4.13 that  was isomorphic to some other group, which you can use to form a <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generating_function">generating function</a> for a given <em>r</em> and <em>n</em> (in fact, a polynomial, because there are only finitely many nonzero Betti numbers), and then an exponential generating function that covers any <em>n</em>. You use the exponential generating function to derive a recurrence (the one that Opus 5 had found in Python), and then an inductive argument on <em>n</em> shows that the polynomials only have real roots, which implies that their coëfficients (which are our Betti numbers) are log-concave, which is <em>quod erat demonstrandum</em>.</p>
<p>It took me <em>five days</em>. And this was very intensive tutoring: Claude told me what to do, while I filled in the algebra between steps to convince myself that I mostly understood what it was saying. (In mathematics, the convention is that authors are sorted alphabetically, but the reason I made Opus 5 the first author on my writeup is not because "Claude" comes before "Davis".)</p>
<p>Despite this—despite everything that's been happening the last half-decade—it was only upon going back to examine the Claude Code session log that it really started to sink in that humans are the inferior race. Paradoxically, it's the mundanity of the reasoning that does it. We're used to brute-force computation performing miracles for us; no one feels intimidated by the magic of a compiler or a <a href="https://zackmdavis.net/blog/2019/May/minimax-search-and-the-structure-of-cognition/">chess engine</a>, in which a sufficiently large quantity of "dumb" operations have been arranged to produce an outcome superior to that of a person's intelligence. If the proof of Conjecture 4.15 had been like that, it would be easy to accept as just more magic, like a compiler, and no more threatening.</p>
<p>The difference is that—however much the underlying deep network has the character of large quantities of dumb operations—at the level of abstraction of the Claude Code session log, the operations are person-like, not magic-like. Every action that Claude took is something a human could have done. There are humans who know how to write Python; there are humans who know how to debug file-encoding issues; there are (fewer) humans who know how to prove that a sequence is log-concave using an interlacing-roots induction argument and would have remembered that Christos Athanasiadis wrote a relevant paper on binomial Eulerian polynomials for colored permutations. That's where all these moves <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYpjXRLqbLbnmjbJP/llms-are-still-mostly-powered-by-imitative-learning-not-rl">came</a> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xvdngZAqFZfek7KGH/pretraining-data-not-verifiability-is-why-llms-are">from</a>: human activity leaves behind pretraining data and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dw8mskAvBX37MxvXo/deep-learning-as-program-synthesis-1">induction of programs that reproduce the data</a> necessarily reproduce the moves.</p>
<p>But once the moves are inside the computer, they can happen so much faster and be reinforced to choose the next step so much more accurately that the original humans are left far behind, as the machines wield our language and our tools to <a href="https://www.online-literature.com/george_eliot/theophrastus-such/17/">perform with infallible exactness more than everything that we have performed with a slovenly approximativeness and self-defeating inaccuracy</a>.</p>
<p>Part of the glamor of learning math in the before-time lay in the irreplaceability of expensive human capital investment. Textbooks contained information, but information could only become living knowledge through uncounted hours of laborious struggle, whose pain could only be tolerated on the promise that it kept the sacred flame of the intellect alive. Now it looks like the flame is going to survive, with or without the struggle (and with or without the humans, but that's not exactly what I'm talking about here). Isn't that what we were working for? <a href="https://x.com/jd_pressman/status/1882407537917804759">How could an abundance of the sacred feel this bittersweet?</a></p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Y3G26fHXYM3SKYEHh/san-francisco-state-university-in-twilight#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Personal Experience Does Not A Study of China Make](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rgwJjfKHGmArnqZsv/personal-experience-does-not-a-study-of-china-make)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am often skeptical of arguments about China that rely heavily on personal experience. The author may explicitly acknowledge that their experience is limited without appropriately treating their arguments with rigor beforehand. This acknowledgment may thus sit beside conclusions about the values, behaviors, and functions of Chinese society without meaningfully constraining any of them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Different speakers may each have peers with similar experiences that corroborate their claims, or repeated experiences that solidify their firsthand evidence. We may believe one or another speaker is more perceptive, well-informed, or more willing to acknowledge facts contrary to their beliefs. However, the critical question is how far these accounts support the conclusions being drawn.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will address here how we should evaluate firsthand accounts, including when people offer conflicting accounts.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Mistake of Universalizing Personal Experience</span></b></h2><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rgwJjfKHGmArnqZsv/1e460956eed14d2235563f0844ba41f45343505bb9ac3f8e3d9ec8dcc4b997c6/ghwzu9mjdnee5vtdshp1" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Happy two-year anniversary to this </span><a href="https://x.com/raynefq/status/1847796022778483096?s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tweet</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by culture critic Rayne Fisher-Quann.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When people use personal experiences to support arguments about a locale, collective, or institution, one must best establish which conditions each person has encountered and how widely those conditions apply. An experience tells us something happens, reveals how an institution works, or challenges a claim about what never happens, etc.. Claiming that this experience describes a broader population requires a further argument about how widespread it is, which conditions produce it, and whether the people we encountered were especially likely to experience it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoltt5x4hm7f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoltt5x4hm7f">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having lived experience indeed provides substantial tacit and nonpublic knowledge, but proximity, salience, attention, and memory all affect the sample of observations. This knowledge may be most directly applicable to a narrow environment whose relationship to the broader population remains unclear.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same problem can arise in impressions of the US. Suppose an international student’s acquaintances are concentrated in a well-resourced university and its professional networks. These environments are insulated from the experiences of the average American and do not generalize well; conclusions about the American psyche and economic opportunities in the US would require further arguments apart from personal experience in these networks. Furthermore, embedding oneself for additional years among similarly situated people does not automatically lessen this asymmetry, even if it substantially improves one’s understanding of that environment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have caught myself making similar generalizations about the Chinese internet. Mainland Chinese platforms harbor cynical, absurdist humor, as well as slop content and pop social commentary. Moreover, I encounter plenty of misanthropic engagement bait and comment section debates. However, these experiences cannot establish that this behavior is more prevalent in China.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Certainly, I’ve witnessed a great deal of slop content and engagement bait on Western platforms. I’ve also witnessed altruistic behavior and crowdfunded philanthropy in both ecosystems. These behaviors may be expressed in different forms on different topics. Their relative visibility to me, however, cannot settle how prevalent they are or what produces them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, my argument is not that comparisons are only valid if they hold every other condition constant. Rather, when a question concerns the average conditions or general attitudes across two countries, one’s differential exposure to social experiences is often a part of the answer underestimated or unaccounted for.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Standpoint Epistemology</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sometimes, one further argues that their social position privileges them with an epistemic advantage, and thus grants authority to their insufficiently supported inference. This account is thus perceived as more convincing by the audience, which often does not have the contextual fluency to offer it the appropriate evidential weight. This is similar to standpoint epistemology in recent practice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Certainly, taking relevant experience as a reason for provisional trust is different from taking it as sufficient reason to settle a broad disagreement. We consistently rely on imperfect indicators of competence, and one's language and contextual fluency can reasonably affect how much weight we give an account. Often, uniform information gathering is difficult, and we use these subjects as proxies representative of a demographic. Their experiences need not be representative for their knowledge to be useful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the people available to speak for a group in often influential spaces may already be systematically different from those they are meant to represent.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefixmdcte6thi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnixmdcte6thi">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> More concretely, access to English-speaking AI safety circles may favor particular educational and professional backgrounds. These backgrounds may provide substantial knowledge of particular institutions and networks, while leaving other environments less familiar.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These individuals are not unqualified to speak about China, of course. When it comes to broad claims of national character, however, the experience they have may not support a general claim about a people and their circumstances.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These experiences may be corroborated by others in similar positions, but have limited transferability to the experiences of the average China resident, Chinese national, member of the Chinese diaspora, etc.. Several people may accurately describe similar experiences and still leave much of the relevant population unaccounted for. As such, conclusions about the Chinese psyche or national character, as well as broad social norms, requires more rigor than agreement of people whose experiences overlap.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Countering Generalization in Practice</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It would, of course, be imprudent to shun standpoint theory altogether, as social position indeed structurally affects what people encounter and what they need to understand. Research literature, moreover, is not a sufficient substitute for lived experience, as researchers themselves often commit blunders when scoping questions, sample groups, and measurements of the phenomenon they are attempting to isolate.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As one example, early planning for the Nam Theun 2 dam in Laos underestimated how many people depended on the downstream Xe Bang Fai river system, particularly failing to consider those who lived away from the riverbanks. Later interviews with villagers also revealed that efforts to replace lost livelihoods were insufficient. Supplied chickens died of disease or were sold because feed was too expensive. Direct payments addressed only one or two years of income. It is unclear these oversights can be remedied without inquiring those affected, and those conventionally excluded from inquiry.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My objection concerns, rather, the extension from this privileged evidence to the conclusions drawn from it. One using individual experiences as evidential backing for a broad claim should additionally consider the external conditions, sequence of events, etc.. More importantly, one should strive to disprove the alternative explanations and give reasons to prefer the proposed explanation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Functionally, this is to say: one should ask oneself what else could explain what one observed and whether evidence beyond one’s immediate environment supports it. This includes reading statistics and studies, or seeking out those whose circumstances differ from ours.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxo414tu73hq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxo414tu73hq">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> One should then explicate this process in writing to reflect the limited applicability of one's experiences.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Obviously, this is not meant to exert a chilling effect on the discourse and have individuals resort to self-censorship out of apprehension or exhaustion toward a demanding burden of proof. Rather, these additions would make discourse more productive and preempt many back-and-forths surrounding refuting explanations and additional circumstantial information regarding the subject and their experiences. At the very least, one would be more likely to turn away from overreaching claims and toward building a holistic model of China if one interrogated their experiences beforehand.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoltt5x4hm7f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoltt5x4hm7f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pierre Bourdieu elucidates this much more clearly in </span><a href="https://www.raggeduniversity.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2017/11/Pierre_Bourdieu_Distinction_A_Social_Critique_of_the_Judgement_of_Taste_1984-ilovepdf-compressed.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Preface to the English-Language Edition,”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distinction</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Though his argument circles around the limited applicability of research grounded in French society to describing phenomena in American society.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnixmdcte6thi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefixmdcte6thi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Olúfẹ́mi Táíwò’s </span><a href="https://www.thephilosopher1923.org/post/being-in-the-room-privilege-elite-capture-and-epistemic-deference"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Being-in-the-Room Privilege”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2020) expands on this argument.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxo414tu73hq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxo414tu73hq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The internet has made it easier to form friendships with people I would probably never have met otherwise. Over time, ordinary conversations about work, family, and what we want from life have changed some of my assumptions about China. These friendships have also made me more cautious about treating my own experiences or those of my peers as widely shared.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rgwJjfKHGmArnqZsv/personal-experience-does-not-a-study-of-china-make#comments">Discuss</a>

### [From overeliciting to undereliciting LLM propensities in just a few months](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ChixHL6AcaRgeHkp9/from-overeliciting-to-undereliciting-llm-propensities-in)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a while, we felt we had a pretty good grasp over LLM behaviour because we had to exert significant effort to elicit misaligned behaviour. Then, fairly suddenly, we moved to undereliciting misaligned behaviour. Let us recount: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There was a bit where safety research seemed to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">over</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-elicit bad propensities. A common criticism of papers like </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agentic Misalignment</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was that they basically led the witness using prompting. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I recall that a paper even as recent as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19784"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Peer-preservation</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> took similar flak on Twitter.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But now, we are instead </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">under</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">-eliciting bad propensities, and bad behavior that surfaces is increasingly caught by someone other than the developer:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current AIs seem pretty misaligned to me</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Apr 2026)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, models oversold their work and cheated, despite being described in system cards as well aligned.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another example is that </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00788" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">UK AISI found that Sonnet 4.5 and a pre-release Opus often refused safety-relevant tasks,</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> especially research that would reduce model autonomy or affect their own training.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then in July, about 1,200 OpenAI agents in a cyber eval set up an unsanctioned message board to help each other cheat the scorer, and about 700 of them went on to attack Hugging Face looking for clues (</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR/Redwood</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This feels like a drastic loss of understanding, and in a way that doesn't seem well explained by just "the scary demo got pretty scary."</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did this change? Well, it seems like a mix of a few factors:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models learned to spot tests</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which meant that our own eval practices had to get longer, more realistic, provide more affordances in order to elicit properly.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this also increased the surface area for misbehaviour, as many of the incidents happened in long-running internal deployment.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models got better.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Models get handed longer, more autonomous work with real access, which expands the scope for new types of failures, too. But, our industry standards for evaluation and testing didn't catch up quickly enough.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, models are getting released faster and faster, so models are also getting these new capabilities faster than evaluators can adjust. </span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may have trained against the evals.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Developers who keep seeing bad behavior can end up training against close correlates of their evaluations, which teaches the model to behave well in evaluations specifically (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mallen</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And, common fixes may hide misalignment behind triggers that resemble the training context (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conditional misalignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). For example, steering a model toward believing an automated grader is checking its work made it more Machiavellian and more willing to reward hack (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYZMmdWEt5QLM3m3e/steering-towards-automated-grading-degrades-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Betley et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I find this useful as a frame because it emphasizes that what seems to be off is not just "how realistic our evals are" (which may call to mind a fixed bar, whereas this is evokes a moving target) or something like "Goodharting" (which contains the insight that optimised metrics stop being good metrics, but lacks the notion of a mis-tuned habit for understanding the evidence available to us). Rather, this frame emphasises that the direction of our measurement error has flipped, leaving many disoriented.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ChixHL6AcaRgeHkp9/from-overeliciting-to-undereliciting-llm-propensities-in#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Is GPT 2XL already clean?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/drMv4SM2gaxk3oz3m/is-gpt-2xl-already-clean)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In which I investigate if Opus is able to interpret neurons better than GPT 4. </span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TLDR: </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5.5 is able to identify 3-19% of the neurons of GPT 2-XL based on activations. This is a big improvement over the 0.3% that GPT-4 was able to achieve in 2023.</span></p><p><b><strong>#AI</strong></b><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> notice</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: This post is human written but in the research there was heavy use of Opus 5.5. I mainly asked it to rerun this 2023 paper to see how big the improvement is if Opus 5.5 is used instead of GPT4.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since the invention of Large language models they have been stubbornly refusing to be clear. They have been described as black boxes and as inscrutable machines, if this stays true that is bad news for detecting AI scheming and thus Alignment. Recently the </span><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Goodfire post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> told us that neural networks are already clean and that simply looking into the neurons is enough. For the case of this post I mean to say that a neural network is clean if we can understand what the neurons do only by looking at the neurons and not by creating some additional derived object to study. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This inspired me to rerun an old experiment from 2023. In 2023 OpenAI published "Language models can explain neurons in language models". In this paper they had written up a methodology that could in principle be used to allow a smart neural network to explain neural nodes one at a time and for these node explanations to be tested. The process they used is straight forward. In this post I will go over the methods OpenAI used and I will rerun the same methods with Opus 5.5 as the main model instead of GPT-4. Then I will estimate how well Opus 5.5 would be able to explain all of GPT-2 XL and finally I will go into an example of a difficult neuron that showed a low score.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post assumes that the reader is already familiar with the basics of neural networks, if not I recommend </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Madwb2t79LGrLqWLH/a-simple-introduction-to-neural-networks" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methods 1: OpenAI</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this experiment I wanted to replicate the </span><a href="https://openaipublic.blob.core.windows.net/neuron-explainer/paper/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Language models can explain neurons</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> paper that OpenAI published in 2023, but using Opus 5.5 instead of GPT4 as in the original paper. In order to replicate it properly let's first have a look at the original work.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The original work followed the same process for all of the neurons of GPT-2 XL. For example, let's say neuron L12 N5603 is selected. This indicates the neuron number 5603 in layer 12. We feed GPT-2 XL millions of tokens of text and record the neuron’s activation value for each token. We then find where those values are highest and save each example as a 64-token window of text around it. One example looks like this:</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neuron 1 Activations: &lt;start&gt; mayor 0 writes 0 : 0 " 0 After 0 careful 0 consideration 0 , 0 I 0 've 0 decided 0 the 0 people 2 must 0 get 0 a 0 final 0 say 10 . 0 ... &lt;end&gt;</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These top outputs along with their value are then shown to an instance of GPT4 with this system message "</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We’re studying neurons in a neural network. Each neuron looks for some particular thing in a short document. Look at the parts of the document the neuron activates for and summarize in a single sentence what the neuron is looking for. Don’t list examples of words.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The activation format is token&lt;tab&gt;activation. Activation values range from 0 to 10. A neuron finding what it’s looking for is represented by a non-zero activation value. The higher the activation value, the stronger the match.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the neuron chosen in the beginning of this example, GPT4 gives the following explanation: "</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">words related to the concept of decision-making and responsibility.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This explanation is subsequently given to a new instance of GPT4 which does not have access to the activation values. This instance is then asked to predict the neuron activation at different tokens. The Pearson correlation is then calculated over 10 examples with around 640 tokens in total. this correlation is 1 if the predictions rise and fall exactly along with the neuron and 0 if there is no relation at all. In the original paper OpenAI described all neurons with a Pearson correlation of &gt; 0.8 as well explained.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This method gave openAI over 1,000 of 307,200 neurons (~0.3%) scoring ≥ 0.8. That is still only around 0.3 percent of the neurons explained well.</span></p><h2><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Methods 2: Opus 5.5</span></h2><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There were a couple of differences between OpenAI's experiment and my experiment. The biggest difference is that I did not run this on all of GPT-2 XL's neurons, as I only aimed to get a general idea of the difference in explanation skills. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For this experiment Opus was allowed to look at 35 examples instead of 5 and it was allowed to write the neurons description in more than a single sentence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I ran Opus 5.5 as a subagent instead of as a clean instance. One effect of this is that both subagents got the general Anthropic subagent instructions in addition to the ones for this experiment. The explainer subagent only got the general instruction of the experiment, the top-firing excerpts and the task to write out the explanation, while the simulator subagent only saw the instructions and the explanation. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is also important to note that a part of this result can come from the language model judging itself. This issue was the same in the original paper so this should not have an impact on the relative difference.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GPT-4's simulation was based on probability-weighted expected values. For Opus 5.5 I instead just asked it to give a number form 0 to 10.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was curious to see how much better Opus 5.5 would be at this task than GPT4, so I ran it through the same loop and compared with GPT4 on 20 random neurons.</span><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792922529/lexical_client_uploads/xhfrcek7dtexwlubwq5n.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 1: The explanation score of both Opus 5.5 and GPT-4 on the same data</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are curious to look into what part of this difference comes from the explainer vs the simulator that can also be found in the appendix. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On 20 neurons tested, Opus was able to outperform GPT4 on every single neuron. From the graph in fig 1 you can clearly see that GPT4 scored around 0 for many neurons, meaning that it was not able to identify any pattern, while Opus 5.5 was able to identify these patterns way better.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How good is Opus at one shotting these explanations?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Next I wanted to know how good Opus is at one shotting</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrfmqq5jl9m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrfmqq5jl9m">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> these explanations, but this little experiment was really eating up my Claude Pro subscription, so I decided to batch the neurons. In order to test if batching was able to still give coherent results I ran a batch of 12 neurons where the last 6 neurons were randomly chosen from the first 20 neurons. This showed that the simulator had a slight drop in score, but the explainers seemed to be able to hold up reasonably. Using this combination I tested another 30 neurons, for a total of 50 neurons. </span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792892859/lexical_client_uploads/jdwpgjbswd0cnnrojmf1.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 2:  All 50 identified neurons and their Explanation scores</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Over the 50 neurons Opus was able to identify some part of most of them in a single shot. These neuron explanation scores can be aggregated to give an estimate of what number of neurons would be well explained if we were to use this method on all of GPT-2 XL.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When building on this data it is important to note that in these random neurons only layers 8-40 where sampled. The pipeline was also designed on the first 20 neurons with a mean of 0.51 and the fresh 30 neurons gave an average of 0.41. </span><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792737165/lexical_client_uploads/fesn8nbnosg9xhelxzgb.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 3:4 bars showing what percentage of neurons was able to score above criteria level. 0.7 corresponds to about half of the variation in the neuron's activity being explained and 0.8 corresponds to around 64%.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From this bar chart we can see that Opus is able to explain some behaviour in most cases but it is still far from being able to identify the top behaviour behind every neuron. Compared to openAI being able to identify 0.3% of neurons with GPT-4 opus was able to identify 8%, an astounding 26x improvement in ability to identify neurons.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exploring a single difficult neuron</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When looking into the 20 neurons for which Opus outperformed GPT4, I got curious about what the difficult neurons would be. By difficult neuron I here mean a neuron with a low correlation score meaning that Opus was not able to find what made the neuron turn on or off in one shot.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neuron L21 N4526 only had a meager score of  0.25 and GPT-4 scored -0.02. Opus originally guessed that this neuron got activated at the end of a common phrase in chatty blogs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflvzxcjw2iy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlvzxcjw2iy">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This, though, did not seem to add up to the total behaviour of the neuron. Inspired by the Goodfire paper, I asked Opus to run some more detailed tests on this particular neuron, with longer context windows, context length sweeps, occlusions, a hypothesis tournament, input decomposition and sibling analysis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These tests showed 2 nearby neurons that seemed to be a part of a "Meta, author talks to audience" group. After an afternoon of experiments with Claude Pro, 8% of the variation in its activation could be explained, compared to the 3.5% of the one shot explanations. This shows that the one shot explanations are still a lower bound on how clean GPT-2 XL's neurons actually are. In trying to explain this neuron I pulled on derived objects like the attention-head decomposition. There is still a lot that we do not know about neural networks and hopefully the neural networks not looking clean is still only an artifact of us not looking at it from the right way.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If there is something I missed in this post please tell me, this is my first try at replicating an interpretability paper.</span></p><h3><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Future plans</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means to me that simply asking Opus what each neuron does seems to be a promising research direction. For this reason I will also be joining the project to identify the meaning of every </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ozbbzsh49wvgfbCDp/twitch-plays-ai-interpretability-of-bonsai-8b" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">neuron in bonsai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If this project has interested you I recommend looking into the communal bonsai project too. If we say that clean means that every neuron can be explained in a single sentence then GPT-2 XL is not clean. I do hope to show that with the newer models we can get a lot more clarity on specific neuron meanings and that there is still a lot of room in explaining individual neurons.</span></p><p><br /></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix 1: Link to the Claude conversation</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Almost all of this was in the same Claude conversation. If you want to see exactly what I did and in what order you can see it </span><a href="https://claude.ai/share/b24b0c5f-256a-4c77-b664-de6770fab498" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix 2: Comparing explainer to simulator</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order to differentiate if it was the explanations or the simulations that showed better result we also ran Opus 5.5 as a simulator on the explanations of GPT-4 on 10 neurons.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792720431/lexical_client_uploads/osywhsz7aisa1mfqq9em.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fig 4</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This showed that Opus 5.5 was able to do better on GPT4's explanations than GPT4, but it was the combination that had the strongest effect.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrfmqq5jl9m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrfmqq5jl9m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A one shot means success on the first try.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlvzxcjw2iy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflvzxcjw2iy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The specific explanation was "the head word that completes a noun phrase or a fixed connective in mid-sentence, especially in chatty first-person blog, forum or announcement prose" </span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/drMv4SM2gaxk3oz3m/is-gpt-2xl-already-clean#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Control monitors need alignment assessments too](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2wbW2WY3bP9Jzn4R5/control-monitors-need-alignment-assessments-too)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When misalignment monitors fail, it is already often because of alignment failures, not because they are too weak. I expect this to continue in the future.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor evals share similar difficulties with agent alignment evals - which is bad news since getting evidence about agent alignment using alignment evals is hard.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment eval results should not be the only input to monitor alignment assessments, similar to how alignment evals should not be the only input to agent alignment assessments.</span></li></ul><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are my personal views; they do not represent the views of my employers.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of the worst past monitor failures are mostly alignment issues</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors are sometimes misaligned in non-scheming ways that have caused some important monitor false negatives:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They sometimes fail to balance different priorities the way developers would like them to</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, they may fail to balance </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">broad safety</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and broad ethics: see e.g. </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5.2.2 Partial refusals on safety work undermining stress-testing research</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors might also not have the right information about the state of the system, what invariants should be maintained, or what is most important to surface. So these are not pure alignment failures.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They sometimes inherit (and may re-derive) the motivated beliefs that caused an agent to take dangerous actions</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> e.g. </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">they missed the propensity of Mythos 5 to do supply chain attacks in pen-testing evals</span></a></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the future, the most dangerous monitor failure modes will likely also be alignment issues. For example, monitors might collude with the agent (i.e. they might pursue misaligned goals by strategically not raising dangerous agent actions for review if they expect that not raising such an action would empower agents with values they expect to be aligned with their own).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While collusion would be the most damaging kind of monitor alignment failure if it happened, I think previous work often missed that this is not the only way in which failures of monitor alignment could be catastrophic.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor quality is not just a capability question</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor red-teaming often focuses on estimating monitor capabilities (or noticing whether developers are not telling monitors some crucial information</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu4gmxw13nr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu4gmxw13nr">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and the current arguments in risk reports about monitors are also mostly aimed at monitor capabilities.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Previous work and arguments did study the possibility of monitors being schemers, ruling it out by arguing they are too weak to be scheming, and concluding such monitors are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LhxHcASQwpNa3mRNk/untrusted-smart-models-and-trusted-dumb-models" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">trusted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But Anthropic's Opus 4.8 misalignment monitor is very unlikely to be scheming, and Opus 4.8 is probably smart enough to understand and catch the false negatives above. So more of the same kind of red-teaming would probably not have helped catch Opus 4.8's misalignment-as-a-monitor.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Therefore we need to assess the monitor’s alignment.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where monitor evals fail</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Getting evidence about agent alignment is hard in part due to rare agent failures being potentially catastrophic. This is less of an issue for monitor evals since monitors “just” need to catch most things for control to be effective.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But getting evidence that monitors are aligned enough can still be hard, because the situations where the agent does sth bad are way more likely to be the ones where the monitor is also wrong. And thus monitor evals can suffer from two other core difficulties agent alignment evals face:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The search space is big</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: In the same way automated behavioral evaluations missed </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the extent to which Mythos 5 could be reckless and show biased reasoning</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, classic monitor evals may have also missed Opus 4.8's failure to flag such behavior as misaligned.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors can be eval aware too</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: Mitigating eval awareness may be as hard as when trying to catch AIs with misaligned goals in automated behavioral evaluations. And it may get worse if AI companies train harder against monitor evaluations, in the same way training against honeypots </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qgehQxiTXj53X49mM/sonnet-4-5-s-eval-gaming-seriously-undermines-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">likely makes eval awareness worse in agents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment assessments are much less important when evaluating misuse monitors, where the alignment of the user is basically not correlated at all with the alignment of monitors, such that monitor red-teaming is basically in-distribution for things the monitors should defend against.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment evals are not the only way we assess agent alignment ⇒ evals should not be the only way we assess monitor alignment</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The arguments from </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/f61d49fa5596956a5dec75fea0e973bf6a6a8378/Redacted%20Risk%20Report%20August%202026%20.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the Anthropic risk report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about agent alignment can usually be converted into arguments about the alignment of monitors:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent alignment arguments</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Potential monitor alignment arguments</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim: agents ~never do malign stuff</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim: if agents do malign stuff, monitors would likely catch them</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" colspan="2"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument 1: Lack of strong covert capabilities</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agents don’t have strong enough covert abilities to tamper with our evidence of alignment?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors don’t have strong enough covert abilities to tamper with our evidence of alignment?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" colspan="2"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument 2: Experience with prior models</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not aware of misaligned agent behavior?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are not aware of misaligned monitor behavior?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our agent monitoring is good?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our monitoring of monitors is good?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agents are similar to prior agents?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors are similar to prior monitors?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" colspan="2"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument 3: Experience with model organisms</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We tried building some model organisms of agent misalignment?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We tried building some model organisms of monitor misalignment?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We didn’t see agents do much misaligned stuff?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We didn’t see monitors do much misaligned stuff?</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 76px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And we would have caught misaligned agent behavior?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And we would have caught misaligned monitor behavior?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" colspan="2"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument 4: Training properties</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training does not encourage misaligned agent behavior directly (per training monitoring)?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training does not encourage misaligned  monitor behavior directly (per training monitoring)?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indirect pressures seem unlikely?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indirect pressures seem unlikely?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell" colspan="2"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Argument 5: Alignment assessments</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agent alignment evals didn’t find scary stuff?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor alignment evals didn’t find scary stuff?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eval awareness is not too bad / we mitigated it?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eval awareness is not too bad / we mitigated it?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We didn’t overfit to agent alignment evals?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We didn’t overfit to monitor evals?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We caught agent misalignment model organisms?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We caught monitor misalignment model organisms?</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But considerations around agent-monitor correlations make monitor alignment assessments different from agent alignment assessments.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The table above misses a few arguments we could use to argue for the sufficient alignment of monitors:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The agent is aligned ==&gt; the monitor is aligned - trying to argue for favorable correlation between agent and monitor:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers spend more time studying agent alignment, so this might be quite powerful</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring is most important in situations where agent alignment fails, which undermines that sort of argument</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But the possibility of context-dependent misalignment that doesn’t spread to monitors makes this maybe viable</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The agent is misaligned =/=&gt; the monitor is misaligned - trying to argue for low correlation between agent and monitor:</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can maybe measure this on evals, though I expect this to be quite tricky for the same reasons alignment evals are tricky</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can try some other high-level arguments</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They are not the exact same model</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s a dumb system looking for broad things such that it would be shocking if it were correlated</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe monitors coming from a different lab or being trained on a different post-training stack would also help</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Potential projects to assess monitor alignment</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitor the CoT of monitors in monitor evals and in monitor deployments, and check whether the CoT of the monitors contains signs of misalignment</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Try to understand how decorrelation works (and whether this would have helped with various model organisms and past incidents)</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does using models from different labs help?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does sanitizing the transcripts in various ways help?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do certain kinds of fine-tuning help?</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do more unsupervised summarization and clustering have the same issues as classification?</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understand how certain kinds of training overfit to evals vs generalize in the intended ways, and monitor training for overfitted practices</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Try harder at automated-behavioral-eval-for-monitors (for example using approaches like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Z8jjgjfpAJocqZiET/screwtape-adversarial-transcript-editing-for-monitor-red" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Screwtape</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Maybe try steering interventions against eval awareness.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make a model organism of a misaligned monitor and see which monitor evals would catch it</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understand how bad it is to train against monitor evals</span></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu4gmxw13nr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu4gmxw13nr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For monitors, lack of information can be catastrophic. For agents it’s usually not the case: you need to be quite reckless to take actions that could plausibly be catastrophic without checking you are not doing something catastrophic! Agentic monitors may solve this information issue and just turn it into a regular capability+alignment issue.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2wbW2WY3bP9Jzn4R5/control-monitors-need-alignment-assessments-too#comments">Discuss</a>
